Grundflow circulation-pump casing and strainer-lid bolt heads are shorter and hexagonal.
- The concrete balance-tank hall’s inlet labels say OLYMPIC POOL / OVERFLOW RETURN 1 or 2, distinguishing the two Olympic returns from pool numbers. The lounge and Olympic pools retain their separate balance tanks.

- The switch room's generator connection point was drawn as a switch-fuse and its main earthing terminal as a placeholder card: the rooms' model table picked exhibits by their place in each list, and an exhibit inserted into the electrical list shifted them. Models are now picked by title, and a test checks no machine with a model falls back to a placeholder. The switch room's title card no longer mentions a UPS it does not have.
- Each boiler in the three boiler-house cascades is individually registered and inspectable under its existing B-501…B-506 plate, including its casing and label. Its live inspection state follows its own controller: unpowered, switched off, standby, purging, igniting, firing, pump run-on, a recoverable low-water-pressure block, or lockout with the actual ignition/flame/overheat cause.

- The spa cabinets' display strip (jacuzzi and foot spa) is light grey, so the LCD's black bezel and the black DISPLAY and MODE buttons no longer vanish black on black in a dim plant room.
- The ozone generator's door gauges (both plant rooms): the vacuum gauge enters from the back, its stem hidden behind the door as a panel gauge's is; both gauges are the same size, each labelled under it (COOLING WATER TEMP, INJECTOR SUCTION) with clearance from its bezel; the rotameter's label, hidden behind the door's face, shows again.
- The Olympic plant room's valves on level pipes (about thirty: the pumps', filters', contact tank's, heat exchanger's and UV's) are drawn along their pipes, not crossways on them; the UV's isolating valves stand on long straights clear of their bends (PL2-UV1-IV2 well up its drop, PL2-UV1-IV1 on the dosing spool's run along the wall).
- The pump pairs no longer draw a non-functional butterfly valve on each pump's discharge riser; their valves are the room's plumbing's, each real and inspectable.
- A lounge valve's overlay said where it was commanded to be, not where it was: while travelling it now reads part open, as its position indicator shows (found by Codex reviewing prompt 069).
- A lounge pump with its own discharge valve shut now has nowhere to pump: it starves and trips, and the standby starts (the owner, 2026-10-25). Before, either pump's open discharge counted for both, so the duty pump ran on against its shut valve and the standby never started.
- The ozone contact tank (PL1-T2, and the ozone room's): its name and its danger warning are spray-stencilled on its curved shell instead of flat engraved plates standing off it; its nozzles come out of its front where the room's pipes meet them (in low left, out high right, the ozone between), so no pipe floats over its dome and no stub sticks out of its side to nothing; its outlet port is lowered from 2.4 m, above the shell, to 2.0 m. A sectional tank's maker's badge (PL1-T1's and every other) stands clear of its front wall instead of 3 mm inside it, so it no longer z-fights.
- The separately installed Warden ozone monitors have permanent catalogue references PL1-GD1 and PL2-GD1, shown on their alarm control boxes and in inspection, copying, inventory and console lookup. ACCEPT and RESET are mounted on a supported Penfold enclosure instead of floating above the detector.

- Circulation pump duty/standby identification is engraved on a solid brass plate screwed to each pump’s flat base frame, replacing floating yellow labels. The lounge balance-tank drain PL1-T1-DV1 discharges across an air gap into a working Certikind floor gully bound to the schematic’s waste node. The dosing controller’s lid now sits above its housing rather than sharing its top face, fixing PL1-CP2’s z-fighting.

- Actuated butterfly valves on upright pipes now mount on their stems; electric and pneumatic overrides remain horizontal.

- Reopening the jacuzzi balance tank’s make-up valve now restores filling: PL3-BT1-IV1 is the source of truth instead of leaving a second saved make-up flag latched off. Older make-up commands operate the same physical valve, and previously stuck saves recover on the next simulation step.
- Fire-collar fixing tabs no longer flicker against walls: corrected their mirrored geometry so the raised outer faces render instead of their backs, which lay directly on the wall.
- The jacuzzi's balance tank's make-up can be shut off: a small lever valve, PL3-BT1-IV1 (`valve-0102`, open in service), on its drop just above the tank. Shut, its float gets no water, so with every valve shut nothing refills the tank (with water in the tub, its main drains still run into it by gravity).
- The jacuzzi's spa overflow to its balance tank is no longer valved (the owner: a gravity overflow must always run, or the gutter floods the deck): PL3-JZ1-IV2 (`valve-0091`) is removed, its asset id retired, and its row with it from the room's valve panel.
- Valve labels no longer float beside their valves: a butterfly valve's tag and name are printed on white plaques wrapped round its blue body, one each side, clear of its lever (`createValvePlaques`), and a motorised ball valve's round its bronze collar (`createBodyPlaques`); the gas valves keep their tags. `wrapOnto`, which bends a flat label round a cylinder, is shared (`wrap-label.ts`).
- A lever valve on an upright pipe shows its state the right way round: its lever lies along the pipe when open and stands out level, across it, when shut (it was a quarter turn out, open across the pipe and shut along it).
- The spa cabinets' plinth no longer flickers where it met their sides: the back and sides now stand on the plinth instead of running down beside it in the same plane.
- Small printed labels stay readable 1.5 m further off: each fades as if the camera stood that much nearer (`LABEL_REACH`).
- The jacuzzi cabinet's thermostat buttons are white, with + and − printed on their caps (pressing in with them), and its 16 × 2 display stands 1.5 cm further left, clear of them; its STATUS legend is printed with the panel, not Dymo tape. Push buttons take a `symbol` for their caps.
- Leaving noclip stands the walker upright at its normal eye height, level: a camera link opened in noclip had left it hovering at the link's height and rolled with its roll.
- Every successful build now advances and records its patch version in `package.json` and `package-lock.json`; failed builds leave the version unchanged. CI persists successful version updates with `[skip ci]`, while tagged releases retain their exact version. The title screen shows the package/build version, commit ID and UTC build date in its bottom-right corner, including development status and uncommitted changes.
- The footbath no longer starts with a false AHU FAULT: the indication now follows its hall's actual air handling and the air plant's power, rather than asking its cabinet for a compressed-air supply it does not have.
- New Game now resets and saves the simulation before entering the footbath, clearing previous drain-downs, outages, trips and changed controls. Every pool starts full at its design temperature with pH 7.4 and normal chlorine, all supplies available and duty circulation running. Reloading the room also clears cached cameras and room-local state.
- The jacuzzi's valves now matter to its machines. Its circulation pump needs its overflow (IV2) and return (IV1) open: with either shut its flow switch stops it, and its dosing pumps with it, and the cabinet's red NO FLOW lamp lights. The jet pump needs its suction and discharge valves (IV5, IV6), the blower its air valve (IV7).
- The jacuzzi drains by gravity: with its drain valve open it empties whatever its pumps do and with its room's power cut, gurgling and pouring into its tundish all the while. Before, cutting the power or stopping the pump stopped the drain.
- The jacuzzi's drain gurgle in its pool hall now comes from the plant room door, a metre up, and has fallen off over the 8.2 m from the tundish behind the cabinet (about 17 dB), so it is always louder by the tundish than in the hall; with the door shut it loses 30 dB more. Before, the hall heard it from nowhere in particular at full level whenever the door stood open.
- The jacuzzi's and foot spa's cabinet doors have louvred vents outside, near their tops and feet, matching the vents of their inside covers, which had none to meet; the PACKAGED SPA SET plate moved down clear of the top vent.
- The live jacuzzi water surface now follows the world's fill quantity, narrowing below its seat, disappearing when empty and showing filling streams as it refills. Its private development-room fill controls remain independent.
- Jacuzzi music now fades along both connecting corridors, including the Olympic stairs and rotunda approach, reaching the amplifier's nominal level in the last metre before the hall. One continuous programme survives room handovers without distance/cone attenuation inside the jacuzzi. The hall hears the plant drain at −30 dB with the door shut, opening smoothly to full transmission. Jacuzzi, footbath and Dev23 drain sounds share the same synthesized gurgle.
- The drain's gurgle is resynthesised from the spectrum of a recording the owner chose: a heaving rumble under 230 Hz, glugs whose resonance wanders 480 Hz to 1 kHz, a sparkle of drops at 2 to 3 kHz and a fizz to 6 kHz, each at the recording's level and pulsing about five times a second, falling away above 6 kHz as it does.
- Plant Room 1's eleven actuated valves are electric, not pneumatic (the owner, 2026-10-24): spring-return quarter-turn actuators in grey housings with a domed position indicator, power lamp, red manual override and cable gland, heard as a geared motor running up, steady and down, with a relay's click and the end-stop's thud. They spring shut when their room loses power, and no longer depend on compressed air. Every actuated valve is now electric (the owner, 2026-10-24): `createActuatedButterflyValve` drives electrically by default, the Olympic room's valves and dev29's actuated exhibits included (dev29's AIR selectors are gone, and its selectors now follow their valves when worked another way); `{ drive: 'pneumatic' }` remains to hand. The jacuzzi's and foot spa's motorised ball valves keep their small ball bodies but carry the same actuator: the grey Grundflow housing, domed position indicator, power lamp and red override, the same motor, relay and end-stop sounds, and spring return, shutting when their room loses power and opening again when it returns (`MotorisedValveState.power`).
- Pneumatic filter valves now sound their solenoid switching, cylinder exhaust during travel and mechanical stops. The shared valve component follows actual position and supply state, including lounge-pool panel commands and spring-return closure on power loss. Hidden rooms stop their held sounds and do not replay old transitions on return.
- The jacuzzi plant room's fibre illuminator console sat over the music rack, so the rack seemed to be labelled SPA FIBRE ILLUMINATOR; the console is now on the wall to the rack's left.
- The jacuzzi cabinet's air blower no longer sounds like its jet pump: it has its own machine tone, `side-channel`, a regenerative blower's hard 2.3 kHz siren over a hiss of air with little low end, against the pump's low churn. Dev7, the listening room, has a stand for it, and places for the two tones that had none.
- Nearest-room preloading no longer constructs speculative scenes or runs their first effects render during play, which caused pauses when approaching rooms the player did not intend to enter. Background requests share their imported code with doorway requests and retry failed imports when needed.
- Cutting the power to a spa's cabinet at its room's switch-fuse, the switchboard or the main switch stops it, as its isolator always did: its pump, its dosing pumps, its UV and its hum stop, its lamps go dark, and its JETS and AIR buttons do nothing. The jacuzzi's wall plate by the tub is dead too while its cabinet has no power: its rings dark, its switches doing nothing, the tub's light out.
- The spa plant rooms' electricity runs in order (the owner, 2026-10-24): busbar, tap-off unit, switch-fuse, isolator, cabinet. The switch-fuse has its supply in and its feed out (two glands, as the isolator has), where it had one; the busbar is plug-in busbar trunking laid by the pipe helper (a new square kind, `busbar-trunking`) as the plumber chooses it for the current, in through the south wall, capped at its far end; its tap-off unit is a component (`createBusbarTapOff`) hung under it over the switch-fuse. Every other room's switch-fuse now has its feed out too, up to its distribution conduit. dev0 shows the line on its south wall, the switch-fuse and isolator working.
- The jacuzzi's and footbath's plant rooms' power conduits came from nowhere (the room's supply down from the ceiling, the cabinet isolator's into the south wall). Each room now has a busbar trunking along its west wall at 3.5 m, in through the south wall, and each conduit rises into a fused tap-off box hung beneath it.
- The boiler house's emergency stop now does something (the owner, 2026-10-24): its gas safety (the ECV, the emergency stop, the methane detector and the safety shut-off valve) is in the world (`game/plant/boiler-gas.ts`, world version 23), so it survives a reload and the control room sees it (BOILER LOCKOUT and GAS DETECTED windows). Struck in, the stop shuts the SSOV, which stays shut after the stop is pulled out until GAS RESET is pressed with every permit made; with the SSOV or ECV shut the boilers get no gas, so the pools and halls get no heat. The board's sounder-beacon is now a working one, flashing and sounding a new invented methane alarm while the stop is in or gas is detected; its fault lamp lights while the SSOV is shut, flashing while the stop is in; and each boiler's display alternates "6A5" (GAS) with its status while no gas reaches it, even idle.
- The Olympic plant room's sample tube beside PL2-P1-IV4 now meets Pump B's discharge pipe through a bolted sampling saddle, reducing boss and compression gland, closing the 80 mm gap that left it looking disconnected.
- The Olympic pump discharge manifold now has a full-bore swept elbow and a socketed 160–355 mm reducer into Pump B's riser, replacing the abrupt small-pipe-to-large-pipe junction.
- The boiler house's power reaches its machines: the boilers, the circulator's two heads and the pressurisation unit have power ports (a gland in each boiler's back, the top of each head's control box, the top of the unit's cabinet), and their conduits now run into them along each port's direction, rather than ending in mid-air at a machine's middle or at an outlet box with a cable to a point beside it. A machine standing in the room is fed into its power ports; a machine by a wall without them keeps its outlet box and cable.
- Labels sit on their machines: the labels and makers' badges and nameplates on the boiler house's cylinders (the expansion vessel, the calorifier, the low-loss header, the dosing pot) follow the curve of the shell instead of standing off it as flat cards; the pressurisation unit's label is on its cabinet, the circulator's tag on its pipe, and the gas train's on its frame and its safety valve, rather than hanging in the air. A port's nozzle no longer flickers where the pipe or conduit meets it (its faces no longer share a plane).
- The boiler house's calorifier no longer has three pipes stopping in mid-air: its cold feed, hot-water flow and secondary return rose to 5.6 m in the middle of the room and ended there, under a services ceiling that does not hide them. They now rise beside the calorifier and run south together at 4.4 m, over the heating mains, out through the south wall in sleeves, as the heating's flow and return to the building do.
- The jacuzzi's and footbath's plant rooms' pipe labels point the way each run flows, not the way it was drawn: SPA OVERFLOW, JETS SUCTION and LTHW FLOW now point into the cabinet. Their labels are stencilled onto the pipes (the service's name and an arrow, upright from either side) rather than hung beneath them on plates, the runs there being packed close; other rooms keep their plates.
- Inside the jacuzzi's cabinet, the jet pump's discharge ran straight through the air blower's air line; it now steps 120 mm toward the jets' bay's end before going back to the wall, and its connection and the room's pipe move with it. A test now checks every pipe inside both spa cabinets, the machines' own pipework included, against every other.
- The stair helper no longer draws level handrails and toe boards over and under its flights (along each flight's outer side and across its ends, at the height of the deck it reaches): the "structure above the stairs" the owner saw in the air plant. A flight's own handrails slope with it; only its sides stop a walker.
- The foot spa cabinet's existing water-temperature mimic now has prominent DRAINING and FILLING annunciators, separate from the small make-up valve-position LED. DRAINING requires its drain line-up, pump on and water remaining; FILLING requires an open valve, float call and live booster. Low water cuts out the cabinet pump and lights a LOW WATER fault; the thermostat +/− buttons change the setting on the first press. Pipe labels on the far side now reverse their arrows so both sides show the true flow direction.
- Turn the footbath plant's draw-down valve lever toward its gully so its open position clears the nearby pipe stand.
- In the footbath plant room, move the draw-down valve off the cabinet's short drain stub and turn and lift the heating-flow valve clear of the neighbouring return rise.
- A packaged cabinet (the footbath's, the Jacuzzi's) stops the walker at its body, not at its layout envelope, which had kept one about a metre short of its doors (the owner, 2026-10-23).
- Seven-segment LED readouts (`createSevenSegment`, on every mimic and many panels) drawn as LED digits (the owner, 2026-10-23): segments with pointed ends and a hair's gap between them, a glow round the lit ones, the dark ones faintly there, at four times the resolution, so close up they no longer read as blocks of pixels.
- From the tank hall, the Olympic plant room is seen through its open doors again: the hall's canvas cleared opaque, hiding the room drawn under it. The balance tank panel moved to the plant room's west wall just north of the tank hall's doors (the owner, 2026-10-23).
- Dev26's flowmeters showed spurious flows while its valves changed over with the supply off: with no head across the network, the solver's unconverged flows leaked through. With the supply off there is now no flow at all.
- A sand filter's sight glass is now a reusable component (`plant/logic/sight-glass.ts`, `plant/views/sight-glass.ts`): a two-dimensional view through the round window at the top of the bed, for filters of any size. It is modelled on what operators look for:
  - in service, clear water over a flat bed, its top crust darkening and thickening with the dirt it holds;
  - left too long, the fouled surface channels into valleys with a ridge at the wall;
  - in a backwash, the bed lifts and churns, air bubbles at first, and the water clouds brown and clears as the dirt washes out;
  - in a rinse, the bed settles back.

  Each quantity eases toward its target, so one state blends into the next. The large sand filter's window shows it live (`showGlass`), driven in Dev26 by the rig's model, and a board in Dev26 shows the five states side by side.

- Dev26 (`?room=dev26`), backwashing a sand filter (scenario 3, prompt 026): one of the Olympic pool's 3 m filters on its five-valve battery (V1 inlet, V2 outlet, V3 backwash in, V4 backwash out, V5 rinse). The valves are actuated, and a Penfold panel switches them together by its MODE selector (OFF, SERVICE, BACKWASH, RINSE), holding the supply off while they travel. The water side is solved as a network with the simulation's solver (`game/plant/five-valve-filter.ts`), so the flow through the bed goes down in service and rinse and up in a backwash. Each valve's branch has a new electromagnetic flowmeter (Kessler-Moody, its LCD showing the flow and its direction). The panel has a mimic of the five valves, the five flows, the bed's top and bottom pressures and their difference, and SUPPLY ON, VALVES MOVING and BACKWASH DUE lamps. The dirty bed backwashes clean, its waste running brown to the gully. A small filter with a top-mount multiport valve (`multiport-filter.ts`) stays as a component; the floor gully's stream can be tinted by how dirty it runs.
- Dev25 (`?room=dev25`), a tank filled and drained through one port (scenario 2, prompt 025): an IBC's single bottom port goes to a tee, with a fill leg from 3 bar mains through a manual valve and a drain leg through another to a floor gully. Which way the water goes through the port follows the valves and pressures. A small network solve at the tee (`game/plant/tank-tee.ts`) balances the mains' inflow against the flows into or out of the tank and down the drain. With the fill open it fills; with the drain open it empties by gravity; with both open most of the mains runs to the drain. Overfilled, it spills from its top and the display says so. Nothing is created or lost.
- Dev24 (`?room=dev24`), duty and standby pumps (scenario 1, prompt 024): two end-suction pump sets on a shared suction header and a common discharge header. Headers are a new kind of component, a manifold with a main end and branch stubs. A Penfold duty/standby panel runs them (`game/plant/duty-standby.ts`). The duty pump runs; if it has not started within 3 s, because its local isolator is off, say, the panel latches its fault and DUTY FAIL and starts the standby; RESET clears the fault once the pump is available. The plumber gives each pump its suction valve, its discharge non-return and isolating valves, and a local isolator. Click a non-return valve to jam it open, and water comes back through the idle pump: its local flow readout goes negative and the delivery falls. The panel shows the run and fault lamps, flow and discharge pressure, and the pumps' gauges read live. The experiment rigs share their fitting placement (`rig-fittings.ts`).
- Eight test scenarios, written as prompts 023 to 031 with their status (`doc/prompts/scenarios.md`).
- Dev23 (`?room=dev23`), draining a jacuzzi: a 1000 L IBC of water on a concrete plinth, its bottom outlet valve piped in DN50 waste to a stainless floor gully. Click the valve's lever and the tank empties by gravity (`game/plant/gravity-drain.ts`). The flow is set by the head above the pipe's open end and the run's losses, so it is fast at first and slows as the water line falls through the bottle's wall. It empties completely, in about five minutes. The drain run falls all the way at 1 in 40 and discharges over the grating with an air gap, where the water can be seen running into the gully. An LCD on the plinth shows the contents and the flow. The IBC is now a shared component (`createIbc`), which the acid tank uses too.
- Dev22 (`?room=dev22`), a battery-backed pump: an endless 450 V experimental supply, through an isolator, to a rectifier/charger, a battery bank and an inverter. These are three new components, the parts of a 40 kVA-class online UPS (`game/plant/power-conversion.ts`). From the inverter, through a second isolator, the supply goes to the end-suction pump set, which pumps water from an endless source to an endless sink. The plumber adds the isolators, the pump's suction valve and its discharge non-return and isolating valves, and routes the conduit and DN150 pipe. Isolate the supply and the battery (4.32 kWh) carries the 32 kW pump for about 7.7 minutes; then the inverter cuts out and the pump stops, restarting when the supply returns. Each cabinet's panel shows volts, amps, kilowatts and status lamps, and the battery's also shows energy (kWh), charge, voltage, current and runtime. The pump's suction and discharge gauges read live, and its local panel shows RUNNING and the flow.
- Experimental sources and sinks show their value and unit on a backlit dot-matrix display (a 16 × 2 character LCD), front and back.
- Dev21 (`?room=dev21`), source to sink: an experimental source of every substance along one wall and its sink facing it across a two-storey room. The plumber joins each source to its sink, sets a valve into each end of every line (a power isolator on power), and leaves them all closed. Open both of a line's valves and its substance flows, at the potential difference over the two resistances, capped by either's maximum rate, until the source is empty or the sink full. The lines rise straight from the studs and cross overhead, clear of the walkway. The plumber has two new options for this: `isolateMedia` (which media's lines are isolated, at both ends) and `normally` (the state its fittings are left in, open by default).
- Experimental sources (`game/plant/experimental-source.ts`): placeholders for a supply of any substance before the real plant behind it is modelled. Always drawn the same way, as a cylinder in the substance's colour banded EXPERIMENTAL, each has typed outputs (as many as asked, each carrying its substance in that substance's medium). Its one control port, `remaining`, only reports the quantity left, a number that may be infinite. It has no switch, since starting and stopping a supply is a valve's job. Every port has a precise place and outward direction in the source's own frame (`SOURCE_SHAPE`): the outputs are studs round its back, and the control port is a cable gland on top. What is drawn downstream runs it down. There is a source for every commodity: pool, mains, dirty, heating, domestic hot and chilled water; natural gas, LPG, chlorine, ozone, carbon dioxide and compressed air; hypochlorite, acid, sodium bicarbonate and flocculant; heating oil and diesel (a new `oil` medium); and 400 V three-phase, 230 V single-phase, 110 V CTE and 24 V DC power. Every source has a matching sink, with typed inputs, a capacity (which may be unlimited) and a read-only `contents` control port reporting what it holds, starting at nothing. Both have a potential (pressure, or voltage), an internal resistance and a maximum flow rate, so nothing flows at infinite speed: flow is the potential difference over the resistance, capped at the rate, and every draw or delivery over a time step is capped too. Each cylinder takes its BS 1710 identification colour and code bands (power is orange, banded in its IEC 60309 voltage colour) and has lettering painted straight onto its curved front and back (its outputs or inputs are upright studs across the top centre, its control port a gland on the back): a source or sink symbol, its name, and its rating (substance, capacity or UNLIMITED, maximum flow, resistance in ohms for power, and pressure or voltage). Dev20 (`?room=dev20`) shows a source and a sink of each.
- Components can come in variants (`DemoModel.variants`). Dev19's pool heat exchanger can be the shell-and-tube exchanger on its plinth, or a plate exchanger on the floor or on a 0.3 m plinth. The plate exchanger has all four ports at its fixed frame's corners, so its movable end opens without breaking a pipe. A new placement-scorer measure, port heights, prefers ports at a working height and marks down any below 0.3 m, which limit routing. Scored end to end, the shell-and-tube exchanger on its plinth wins: it is the only variant that passes every gate (85), and the most compact.
- A clash-free router (`voxel-router.ts`, by Codex from prompt 021) replaces the old overhead lanes. It searches a grid round the reserved regions and the lines already routed, and proves every route with the clash checker. Lines leaving or reaching the same port may share their way out, as a bus (power feeds from the mains) or a header (a pump feeding a battery of filters). The scorer counts overlaps within such a group as shared, not as clashes. The dearest pipes route first, and the flue before them all. Dev19c is the first Dev19 to pass every gate: every line routed, no clash, every fitting within reach, scoring 84. Four routing approaches were tried side by side (`router-trials.ts`: rack levels, voxel search, voxel with direction levels, rip-up and reroute) before the merge.
- Dev19's heat exchanger is now a shell-and-tube pool heat exchanger after Bowman's FG100 (`shell-tube-exchanger.ts`), in place of the plate exchanger. Pool water goes straight through its end covers, and the boiler water enters and leaves by two branches on top. It stands on a concrete plinth, so the pool line passes through it at 0.9 m and the heating pipes drop onto it from above, with nothing over it. It takes 0.6 × 0.32 m of floor against the plate exchanger's 1.3 × 0.8 m.
- Dev19d (`?room=dev19d`): Dev19's plant with three sand filters in parallel (`demo-variants.ts`), made by every stage.
  - **Blocks:** the filters are pre-assembled into candidate blocks, and a row of three in the island wins. The sizer now leaves room for the longest freestanding block to stand in the island.
  - **Headers:** where several lines share a port, such as the pool pump's discharge feeding all three filters, the plumber installs its fittings once on the common pipe, with a header node after them that the lines branch from. Flow passes through headers.
  - **Graph:** each fitting keeps one identity, and the plant runs on the plumbed graph. Shutting the pump's one discharge isolator stops all three filters.
- Placement follows three rules of process plant layout, from N. S. Nandagopal's _Practical Process Plant Layout and Piping Design_ (IDC Technologies, 2009):
  - **Process order:** equipment follows the process flow diagram, the process going one way round the room.
  - **Short pump suctions:** a suction counts double, so each pump stands by the tank it draws from.
  - **Withdrawal space:** room to draw off a pump's motor, open a plate exchanger's pack, and lift a filter's internals out.

  The placement scorer measures the first two. Dev19c is now the best of sixteen placements with these rules (seed 9). It scores 74.1 against 72.2 for the best before them, and its process order rises from 51 % to 84 %.

- A pre-assembled block of parallel units now carries its own pipework and valves. Its headers and branches are made in the shop (estimated at 60 % of the site rate), and it meets the main at a single field joint, where loose units need one each (estimated at £150). Every make-up is priced the same way: a main at the header's size to the group's middle, then a branch to each unit, every length the sum of its x, y and z runs, never a diagonal. For four filters the 2 × 2 block's own pipework costs £1,333, against £2,143 for four loose units.
- Identical units working in parallel are pre-assembled into candidate blocks before placement (`unit-blocks.ts`): for four filters, a row of 4, a 2 × 2 square (two rows back to back, standing across the island's two faces), two pairs, and so on down to singles. Each block is placed as one item and expands back into its units, side by side and aligned by construction. A block is piped to what it serves as one, through its header. The placement scorer picks the make-up, rewarding fewer placements and groups standing near what they serve. Four filters come out as a 2 × 2 square in the island, in a 13 m room against 14.5 m for two pairs.
- Pipework is priced (`pipe-costs.ts`), from a table of approximate installed costs per metre for each kind and size of pipe, conduit and flue: steel above plastic, lagged above bare, bigger above smaller. The scorer now judges pipework by what it costs, not by its length. Placement pulls machines together by what their pipe costs. A placement scorer runs straight after placement, before any plumbing, scoring access, sites, batteries, estimated pipework cost and room area. Dev19c's search places sixteen candidates, ranks them by placement score, and plumbs and fully scores only the best four. Machines of the same kind now stand together as a battery: three identical sand filters cluster in the island, where without the rule they scattered. At these estimated rates, Dev19 this morning had about £49,600 of pipework; Dev19c has about £23,900.
- A scorer, the last stage of making a plant room (`room-scorer.ts`), shaped by Codex's advice. It checks gates first: every machine reachable on foot, every fitting the rules want installed within reach, no run cutting into another, and none through a reserved region. It then scores the room from 0 to 100 on access, fittings, clashes, route economy and room area, measured against baselines the room cannot choose, saying why for each. Dev19 this morning scores 39.5; Dev19 now 66.2. Dev19c (`?room=dev19c`) is the best of twelve placements by the scorer, at 67.9. All of them still fail on pipe clashes from the old overhead lanes. The scorer also caught the boiler's power drop running down through the ring walkway, now fixed.
- The plumber now fits Dev19's valves and isolators itself (`doc/prompts/done/018-plumber-valves-and-isolators.md`). It adds isolating valves at pumps, filters and the heat exchanger, and a power isolator at every powered device, the boiler beyond its breaker included. It sets them and the remote valves into their lines, never on the floor, and only where someone standing on clear floor can reach them, reporting any it cannot. Each line leaves its machine first along a straight at a reachable height, long enough for its fittings. Hand valves have levers coloured for what they carry. The flue now ends at a magic ceiling terminal straight above the boiler. Dev19b (`?room=dev19b`) is the same room with the rules Codex proposed on the old overhead lanes, kept for comparison. The simulation does not yet read the fittings.
- A plant room can now be laid out by layers (`doc/prompts/done/017-room-sizer-gantries-placement.md`). A room sizer sets its width, length and height from what it holds; a regions helper reserves its door approach and, from two storeys up, an observation gantry and a stair against one wall, where no pipe may run; and a placement helper stands the machines in bands, backs to the walls or back to back in an island, all facing a ring walkway. The big tanks go in corners, panels and signs on walls, and pumps and vessels freestanding.
- Dev19 is the first room laid out this way: 13 × 13 m and 8 m tall instead of 20 × 20 × 4 m, with a walkable stair and gantry, yellow walkway lines and the pipe-free space shown faintly. Its piped machines stand much closer together. Its pipes are still routed by the old lanes, pending the new plumber.
- Dev19's physical port graph now produces a weighted machine adjacency matrix for compact placement. The pool mimic has a temperature target and a heating switch that commands the boiler and primary circulator.
- Dev19's small pool now has a post-mounted water quality sign with live graph-bound temperature, pH and free chlorine readings. Each motorized shutoff and breaker also has a local override button.

### Changed

- The museum halls draw their ambient occlusion at 3 samples a pixel, not 12 (the owner, 2026-10-28); a room tuned below 4 keeps its own.
- The DMH's fascia and everything on it scaled to 95 % about its middle, and its control module moved a little right and narrowed, so module and fascia sit clear of the gearbox with a margin all round (the owner, 2026-10-28: the fascia was clipped by the body at its bottom left).
- A brand's mark reversed out in cream wherever it sits on a ground that would hide it (`markColours`, `drawBrandMark`'s `ground`; the owner, 2026-10-28: Grundflow's impeller was lost on its own blue badge): its makers' plates, its nameplates' bands, the framed tanks' plates, the lorries' doors and the rack units. The DMX 1602's and DDS 7.5-16's keys centred on their fascias (the stepper's a little narrower), the DMH's too, and their Grundflow plates set inside the fascia's dark panel with a margin.
- The DMX 1602, DDS 7.5-16 and DMH keys are plain grey membrane keys (`membrane-key.ts`, about 14 × 10 mm, a darker border and a highlight; START's green bar, STOP's red), their legends printed on the fascia above them (the owner, 2026-10-28: "can you make the buttons grey"; "Put the symbol above"); each press dips the key with a light thock and the pump's piezo sounder (mission 100): a chirp, a confirm on START, the error bleep on a refused key or a fault appearing, Grundflow's DMX and DDS high, the DMH mid. Dev44's trial DMX is labelled DP-101A, as the Olympic's.
- `world.slow.test.ts`'s bathers test counts the morning without hypochlorite against the same morning with it, from the same draws of the bathers' generator, rather than against another day's, which any change in another pool's water moved.
- Slow tests run only locally (the owner, 2026-10-28): the tests over about 1.5 s, or simulating long stretches of world time (the mains water off, the bathers, the air handling, the Wash-O-Tron's AUTO washes, the drain-down, the cooled pool's heating, the layout scorers and the inspection coverage), are moved into `*.slow.test.ts` files, which `vite.config.ts` excludes when `CI` is set, so GitHub's checks stay fast; `npm test` still runs them all, and `npm run test:fast` runs the fast set alone.

- Restored the simple text title menu in place of the Penfold button box, keeping the attract sequence and the New Game, Continue, Settings and Instructions actions.

- The OT-216 terminals and their control box are Chlorella Chemicals' (prompt 091; the owner, 2026-10-28: "specialist Chlorella equipment for automating the distribution of chlorine around a plant"; a deliberate exception to Penfold's control panels, as instruments keep their makers): each terminal a pale green moulded clamshell of the 2000s, slightly rough, two halves meeting at a parting line, rounded corners, screw bosses and a slightly domed front, under one flat, smooth, darker green printed polyester fascia carrying Chlorella's logo, the keys printed patches of it (no modelled or moving keys: hit areas on the fascia, `fasciaKey`; their raised patches, embosses and the LCD window's edge a bump map with a matching roughness map, drawn once and shared, `fasciaMaps`), the LCD behind a clear window, the lamps printed LED windows; `CHLORELLA OT-216` on power-up. The box's enclosure is Chlorella's green, its tag plate green, with Chlorella's maker's and rating plates; the bought-in parts inside keep their makers' badges.
- The chemical distribution's control box looks like a control box (prompt 091; the owner, 2026-10-28: labelled up, two serial inputs from the terminals entering through glands, switching gear inside): an engraved tag plate on its door (its tag, CHEMICAL TRANSFER CONTROL, HYPOCHLORITE · ACID), the electrical danger sign (ISO 7010 W012), a FED FROM plate and Penfold's rating plate; a label tied on at each of its five glands (`BOX_GLANDS`: OT1 HYPO RS-485, OT2 ACID RS-485, SUPPLY 230V AC, LEVEL SIGNALS, OUTLETS & PUMPS), each gland with its locknut; inside, the two terminals' violet serial cables up from their glands to the comms module's ports (P1 OT1, P2 OT2), the supply's cable and the field multicores to the terminal rail. In dev44 the terminals' conduits now reach the box's glands.
- The OT-216 chemical distribution terminal is 20% bigger (prompt 091; the owner, 2026-10-28: "as designed, 20% bigger", so its LCD reads more easily): its case about 240 × 168 × 60 mm (`OT216_SCALE`, `OT216_CASE`), everything on it scaled with it.
- Displays redraw only when what they show changes (mission 094, from the component benchmark; behaviour and appearance unchanged): the control room's circular chart recorder inks and uploads its 512 × 512 chart only when a pen has moved a pixel (it uploaded every frame, 15.7 M texels a second); the DoseCell heads compare what they show before building their dot frame (165–195 µs a frame each to 17–29 µs); the plant reading the control room and plant rooms show is worked out once per world step and shared (`plantReadingOf`); the Olympic balance tank panel's view once per step (146 to 8 µs); the alarm printer's paper reshaped only when it moves (129 to 2 µs); the diaphragm dosing pumps' LCDs paint only the dots that change; the mimic boards set a lamp's colour only when it changes. Measurements in `doc/reports/component-cost-2026-10-27.md`.
- Mission 094, further down the ranking (2026-10-28; behaviour unchanged): view state worked out once per world step, not by every display every frame: the model's part index (`identifiedPart`, which walked the whole model on every call; the foot spa's cabinet did so a dozen times a frame), the Olympic battery's flows (`olympicFlowsOfWorld`, for its five sand filters, its meters and the overviews) and the lounge filter mimic's state; a mimic board sets its lamps and readouts only when its state changes. Updates a frame: the lounge pump panel 139 → 35 µs, the foot spa's cabinet 269 → 58, the jacuzzi's 111 → 25, an Olympic sand filter 198 → 14. A sand filter's sight glass starts settled on its bed's state when its room is built (it eased from a clean bed for half a minute, redrawing 13 times a second: 0.87 M texels a second each, now none); POOLMON's cursor blinks on a layer of its own (the screen re-uploaded 640 × 500 twice a second for it: 0.53 M texels a second, now none while nothing changes). The component benchmark fails loudly: a component over budget is an error in the console, a red banner and the page's title, unless listed with its reason in `KNOWN_OVER_BUDGET`; it names the textures a component uploads. The rule that displays redraw only on change is in `CLAUDE.md`.
- Mission 094, the rest of stage 4 (2026-10-28; [report](doc/reports/component-cost-2026-10-28.md)): small seven-segment displays draw at the large ones' texel density (at least 8 texels a millimetre, not four times over whatever their size), so the spa UV's hours counter uploads 432 × 160, not 864 × 320, each second (the spa and jacuzzi cabinets 690k → 172k texels a second); the control room's mimics, the Olympic pump panel's and the air plant's work out their state once per world step; the boiler house's own model stands still with the world's clock (the menu, the console's `pause`), so its displays upload nothing then. The component benchmark takes an update's cost as the median of six batches and measures again a component the page was starved of frames for, so a budget fails on the component, not on the machine; the full run passes its budgets, every overrun listed with its reason.
- The dosing pumps rebuilt to the peristaltic pump's standard, still diaphragm pumps (prompt 089, item 2, the owner, 2026-10-27): the hypochlorite set's duty and standby pumps are Grundflow DMX 1602 solenoid pumps (after the ProMinent gamma/ X, 2.1 l/h, 180 strokes a minute) and the acid board's a Grundflow DDS 7.5-16 stepper pump (after the Grundfos SMART Digital DDA), each a rounded clamshell with a parting seam on rubber feet, its PVDF dosing head with its bolts, suction and discharge valves on union nuts and a bleed knob, a membrane fascia with a 5 × 7 dot-matrix LCD (its rate in l/h, AUTO or MANUAL, its percentage and strokes a minute), working START, STOP, ▲, ▼ and P keys, STROKE, WARN and ERROR lamps, the stroke lamp flashing and the head's diaphragm cover pulsing with each stroke, the solenoid's tick or the stepper's whirr while it runs (`diaphragm-pump.ts`, its controller `logic/diaphragm-pump.ts`). They dose as the room's controller asks, on the room's power; the standby stands. Their boards and ports are unchanged. Dev44 shows each pump on a plinth beside the peristaltic pump, and the hypochlorite set and the acid board on stands.
- The lounge's dosing put right (prompt 089, item 4, the owner, 2026-10-27, the Olympic's way): the controller samples the filtered main through a saddle before any dosing (it sampled between the two injection points); the acid goes in after the heat exchanger, on the heated main's riser, and the hypochlorite last, after the UV unit, on the return main's riser (it went in before the ozone contact tank, 0.3 m from the acid). Each through a drawn injection quill set (`injection-quill.ts`: PVDF quill in a clamp saddle, a DN15 non-return valve and a DN15 isolating ball valve, 1.5 m up), each quill a node of the schematic and each valve a fitting of it (PL1-IQ1-IV1, PL1-IQ2-IV1); shutting a quill's ball valve stops that chemical going in.
- The footbath's hypochlorite day tank stands in the plant room's south-west corner, backed onto the south wall and facing north, clear of the south door's swing and leaving a 1.1 m way into the cabinet's lane along the west wall; its fill and dosing tube follow it (prompt 089, item 1, the owner, 2026-10-27).
- Chemical dose-bottle bunds have separate red/yellow rim caps above their black walls, with fitted corners, removing the overlapping faces that caused z-fighting.

- Document mission filing rules and audit all 85 mission prompts: mostly completed missions now live under `done/`, with dated evidence and outstanding work retained in the prompt and TODO.

- The wet hall's booster set lined up with the break tank's outlet (the owner, 2026-10-26): the outlet low on the tank's east end at the booster's suction height, the booster's suction on the same line, a flowmeter's room left between them for a meter planned on the tank's outlet side (`OUTLET_METER_GAP`). The tank's mains inlet stays on its roof, its Category 5 air gap inside it.
- The wet hall's mains come out of the north wall (the owner, 2026-10-26: the outside world's services come in by that wall): a flanged stub of the town main and of the boosted make-up main out of each marked sleeve, for the plumber to join. The make-up line moved 2.5 m east along the bay (`lineShift`), the town main's sleeve staying put, leaving room at its west end for its pipes and an easy elbow from the main.
- The wet hall's make-up line in the water regulations' order (the owner, 2026-10-26), west to east along the bay's north wall: the town main in at the west end, through the utility's meter and our own flowmeter into the break tank across its Category 5 air gap, the booster set drawing from the tank, and the boosted make-up main out at the east end, high, to the pool plant rooms. Its two connections through the north wall are marked by sleeves and plates, TOWN MAIN IN and MAKE-UP MAIN TO POOL PLANT ROOMS, the schematic's boundary points for the plumber.
- The wet hall's make-up and pit, further (the owner, 2026-10-26): the utility's meter is read by its dial, not wired into (no pulse emitter), its Splashonomics mark wrapped round its register (`createBrandBand`), its run's valves and strainer left to the plumber, its stubs shortened; our own Kessler-Moody electromagnetic flowmeter (WH-FT1) after it, wired to a new make-up water recorder (WH-FQR2), a pen plotter of the water used, kept in the world (world version 36); the line runs break tank, meter, flowmeter, booster set's suction, straight. The backwash pit is railed round (a gate toward the aisle), its davit outside the rail, its level transmitter under the gratings, its connections its own (in through the gratings, the pump's riser through the hatch, the overflow and drain below the floor), its walls' tops and frame no longer flickering against the slab.
- The wet hall's make-up bay (the owner, 2026-10-26): its partition the hall's own breeze block at its walls' thickness, 3.6 m tall, running through to the east wall; LED battens on both its faces at the hall's height, with trunking on its aisle side; the standard pair of equipment doors (MAKE-UP WATER, authorised personnel). The hall's own batten where the partition meets its east wall is left out (`partitionsMeet`), as is any batten a control-bus conduit would cross.

### Fixed

- The control bus ran across the doors of a room whose floor is below its doors (the wet hall's, half a storey down) and through every door's lintel: it now rings the room clear of its lintels (0.35 m over the door heads, `OVER_LINTEL`) at the level its doors open from (`deckOf`).
- The wet hall laid out afresh to the owner's plan (prompt 036, 2026-10-26), before any plumbing: a protected make-up bay to the north behind a 3 m blockwork partition with its own service door, holding the GRP break tank, the booster set, the meter and the clean side's mimic; a clear aisle from the east stair to the west ramp; and the backwash pit below the floor to the south, a real opening in the hall's slab (`slabAround`), its concrete walls, floor and settlement sump, its gratings flush with the floor, its sump hatch and pump davit, its level transmitter and staff gauge, its water at the world's level. The outfall monitor moved to the dirty side's east wall. The hall's old pipework is cleared while it is laid out (`BEING_LAID_OUT`, which the plumbing checks pass by).
- The wet hall's backwash holding tank is modelled as its concrete pit (prompt 036; the owner, 2026-10-26; world version 34): sized for two of the Olympic's filters and one of the lounge's washed together (about 49 m³ in eight minutes, measured in the world; 70 m³ by the plant sizing), then half as deep again for water slides and a children's pool to come: 7 × 5 m, 3.0 m of water, 105 m³. At its high-high level its inlet shuts, inhibiting every backwash and rinse to waste until its pump has it back below 75 %.
- The spa cabinets' motorised valves wait for their controller (`cabinet-boot.ts`, `servoCommanded`): powered only while it is on, none opening while it boots or runs its self test, then one after another as it starts, and driven shut on a fault (the owner, 2026-10-26: the jacuzzi's and the foot spa's opened before the self test was done).
- The spa cabinets' panel: a WASH NOW button starts the filter's wash at once; MODE is WASH MODE, its first press showing the wash's page and each press after stepping its schedule (it had changed a page not on show); the thermostat's + and − stand straight under the SET lamp; the display and its three buttons sit evenly along the strip.
- The jacuzzi music rack's units name themselves on inspection with their state: the source what it is playing ("Playing “…”", or Off), the amplifier as an active component (No power, Off, or On at its volume); an untagged component with a state of its own shows it after its name.
- The Olympic plant room's gantry and landing are decks to the pipe supports: what is under them hangs from their underside, nothing hung from the roof passes through them; the east busbar drops and the ozone monitor's supply step in to the wall and pass the deck close against it (the owner, 2026-10-26: a conduit may pierce the gantry only so).
- The Olympic pool's main drains have an isolating valve into the balance tank, PL2-T1-IV1, open in service: shut, the pool holds its water when its pumps stop; open with them stopped, the pool drains into the tank by gravity until it overflows (the owner, 2026-10-26).
- The spa cabinets' servo valves are small, fast geared motors (the owner, 2026-10-25): their whine is higher and quicker (860 Hz, a 47 Hz gear flutter), their start-up servos travel in 1 s and their filters' wash servos in 3 s.
- Plant one's and plant two's filters are the ideal filter (world version 31): the lounge's two Ø1.2 m beds and the Olympic's five Ø3 m beds load from their pool's suspended solids, fissure when loaded and left, lift and wash out in a backwash, settle in a rinse or back in service, and what they let past sets their pool's water; each pool also takes a background load of dust (0.3 g/m² an hour). The Olympic's waste main is sized for a proper backwash (about 180 m³/h, 25 m/h over a bed), so a backwash now heals a bed's fissures. A never-backwashed battery fouls to capacity and breaks through, clouding its pool.
- Equipment inspection headings now use `id (brand model equipment type) - current status`, omitting unknown fields and putting the location/purpose on the second line without repeating status. Flowmeters use their displayed reading as status; the boilers’ HW 300 model identifier is shared with their rating plates.
- Generated equipment descriptions use a definite article, sentence-case type and full location: “The only calorifier in the boiler house”, “The 3rd boiler in the boiler house”, and “The 2nd pump in Plant Room 1”. Technical acronyms retain their capitals; overlay, inventory, manual and console share the wording.
- The Olympic plant room's heat exchanger stands on a 0.6 m galvanised steel frame (`machine-stand.ts`; the plan's new `stand` height, used by its ports and its placing): re-routed, none of its four pipes runs within 0.5 m of the floor, all four of its valves are within reach, and the room's pipework costs about £350 less.
- Each circulation pump's identification is an engraved white laminate plate ("P-201A / DUTY") on the flat face of its motor's terminal box, away from the cable gland, where it is seen, instead of a brass plate low on its base frame.
- A spa filter's wash sends its pump's water to drain from the spa's balance tank, made up by its float, and nothing to the spa; each pool's water carries its suspended solids (world version 30), brought in by bathers and taken out by its filters, the spas' through their own beds, which take their dirt from it. The spas' first wash falls at 02:00, then on their schedule.
- The spa cabinets' filters are in the world (`spaFilters`, world version 29): each bed takes the spa's dirt, its wash programme runs on its schedule or WASH NOW, the spa's circulation stops while it washes; the cabinets' displays and MODE buttons work them, and the filters standing beside the cabinets show their gauge, sight glass and servo as the world has them.
- The spa cabinets' sand filters stand outside them, beside their cabinets (the owner, 2026-10-25): the jacuzzi's Ø0.6 m on a 1.0 m bed, the foot spa's Ø0.5 m on 0.8 m, each in its room's schematic (PL3-F1, PL4-F1) with lever isolating valves at the cabinet, pipes over head height to its unions, and its wash to a tundish beside it; the cabinets keep the pump, UV, heat exchanger, dosing and controls.
- The spas' circulation slowed for sand filters that fit their cabinets (the owner, 2026-10-25, option 2): the jacuzzi's from 24 m³/h (28.3 in its trial) to 8, the foot spa's from 12 to 6; their filters Ø0.44 m on a 0.8 m bed and Ø0.38 m on 0.6 m, both at about 53 m/h. The jacuzzi's balance tank, sized by its allowances, is now 1.1 m³ (its pump's reserve smaller); with its overflow shut it trips on TANK LOW before its gutter floods the deck.
- Prompts sorted again (2026-10-25): 013, 060 and 062 moved to `doc/prompts/done/`; 001, 002, 011, 012 and 016, superseded, to the new `doc/prompts/obsolete/`, with an index of what superseded each; every link to them updated, and `TODO.md` corrected (the switch room's machines, emergency power superseding 011) with items added for prompts 007, 008 and 015's remaining work.
- The walker is 0.35 m wide (the owner, 2026-10-25), down from 0.5 m, a comfortable fit through a gap of that width; layouts still design ways between machines at least 0.5 m clear (`doc/room-layout-guide.md`). A pump pair stops the walker at each pump set as drawn rather than at the pair's whole layout envelope, so one can walk between duty and standby (0.7 m clear in both the lounge and Olympic plant rooms, tested).
- Finished prompts moved to `doc/prompts/done/` (2026-10-25): nineteen delivered prompts (003, 004, 006, 009, 014, 017 to 021, 024 to 027, 032, 038, 041, 043 and 056), with an index of the evidence for each and every link to them updated; the unlisted prompt 010 added to `TODO.md`.
- The jacuzzi's cabinet is heard running its jet pump and its air blower, each while it runs, as well as its circulation pump; and a spa cabinet's shut doors hold its sound 15 dB down, not 20, so its machines are still heard (the owner, 2026-10-24). The jacuzzi has a motorised drain valve on its valve panel: opening it drains the tub to waste, pumped by its circulation pump, heard gurgling into its tundish while the water runs away.
- The jacuzzi's plant room works like the foot spa's (the owner, 2026-10-24: its hand valves were too hard to reach in so tight a room): its seven isolating valves are motorised ball valves, each with its limit lamp and its gear motor heard as it moves, worked from a Penfold valve panel, JACUZZI VALVES, on the north wall beside its mimic.
- The switch room keeps only what works ([043](doc/prompts/done/043-switch-room-tidy.md), the owner, 2026-10-24): its motor control centre, sample rotary isolators and spare distribution board, none wired to the world, are gone (motors are to be started from panels in their own plant rooms, [044](doc/prompts/044-plant-room-motor-panels.md)); the switchboard's MCC-1 way is DB-WET HALL, the wet hall it always fed; the trunking runs from the main switch along the walls into the switchboard; the room is laid out by hand where everything else already stood.
- PL2-F1-V4's actuator faces the accessible south aisle. Upright actuated butterfly valves keep their manual override button facing horizontally when the actuator turns sideways.
- Dev19 now occupies a 20 m square, 4 m high room with equipment on all four walls. Its graph service runs use the commodity-based pipe helper, including yellow gas lines, heating pipework, pool and mains water, power conduit and twin-wall flue. Small graph port labels face the player.
- Small component legends, maker badges and machine nameplates fade smoothly as their lettering shrinks below readable screen size, including when the player zooms out.
- Dev19's routed physical services leave their declared graph ports square before the first bend.

### Documentation

- Sized a two-storey lounge pool plant hall from its current inventory and the room-layout pipeline: 116.4 m² calculated floor, a 12 × 11 × 8 m first trial envelope, and a 12 × 10 × 8 m compact candidate to prove by placement and routing (`doc/plans/lounge-plant-room-sizing.md`).
- Defined the visual, simulation and control component triplet, with an inventory-led migration path for the full component catalogue, shared identity, typed ports, saved state and build validation.

### Fixed

- The remote-operated valves in Dev19 were drawn about four times too big. They are now ordinary ball valves sized to their own line, each with a small rotary actuator on its stem at the size of Belimo's 5 Nm and 20 Nm actuators (118 × 71 × 85 mm and 141 × 93 × 86 mm, from Belimo's datasheets). The gas shut-off is a solenoid valve with its coil on top. Each stands on a small channel stand, with its manual override on the actuator. Dev19 was laid out again with them and is now 12.5 × 12.5 m.
- The circulation pumps' orange coupling guard no longer cuts into the motor: volute, bearing bracket, coupling guard (now perforated, on two legs from the base deck), the motor's smooth drive-end shield, finned frame and fan cowl now follow one another along the shaft, laid out and checked by `end-suction-pump.ts`; the motor stands on pedestals and its terminal box on a boss.
- Dev19 now opens with a small pool circuit, balance tank, pool pump, sand filter, treatment cabinet and plate heat exchanger alongside the boiler loop. The trial uses mains electricity and gas, graph-bound remote valves, a gas flow meter with flow and accumulated-volume readings, and the shared pipe helper for its visible services. This also repairs the temporary Vite parse error that prevented the room loading.

- Dev19's pump sound now builds from a softer motor hum, casing vibration, fan and water noise instead of prominent pure tones. Its boiler starts with a blower and igniter, develops a layered flame and flue sound, then closes its gas valve while the blower winds down.

- Dev19's pump and boiler react to power loss; the pump sound winds down and gas and water valves fail closed.

- Dev19 uses motorized gas and water valves and a motorized boiler breaker in place of its manual devices. The mimic commands each one and shows its actual state. Its pump runs up and coasts down audibly with simulated power; the boiler has ignition, firing and shutdown sounds, and motorized devices sound when they actuate.

- Dev19 has a Penfold Controls mini-plant mimic at the requested viewpoint. Its eleven readings and five commands use typed graph control edges; a trunked run marks the shared ceiling data bus.

- Dev19's boiler uses its working control panel: its keys and knob change a separate control state, and its screen, lamps and gauge show readings from the port-graph simulation. The boiler graph node now declares optional typed temperature and setpoint control ports, with read/write validation.

- The switch room distribution board now has a visible circuit chart, an asset label, and an operable glazed door; the same board is available beside the switch-fuse in dev0. The strip-chart recorder's paper, pens, and glass have distinct depths to stop distant flicker.
- The water network solver no longer stalls short of balance on a chain of short pipes high above its datum: its Jacobian steps a fixed distance, not a share of the head.
- Overhead chain-operated room valves now have one operator wheel on the valve spindle, without a second intersecting lever. Room-pipework valve tags sit on both clear sides of the body; two lounge backwash valves now sit on their assigned pipe runs.
- Clicking the inside cover of an open packaged Jacuzzi or footbath cabinet door closes that same door.
- Room-pipework valve controls now use their permanent installed asset IDs for saved actions and show their display tags beside the valves.
- The underwater gallery's door to the engineer's office stands on a 0.25 m sill above the corridor's fillet, which now runs unbroken beneath it (the office's floor meets the sill, as plant rooms do).
- The Olympic pool's lane rope sways gently, as a plucked string in a slow standing wave (about 6 cm at its middle, a 7 s period), still at its hooks.
- The Olympic pool has one lane rope, the middle one, instead of one between every pair of lanes.
- The short (1 m) corridor straights at the pool halls' doors have no ceiling lamps.
- The corridor between the Jacuzzi and the footbath is now a short straight, the rotunda, and a short straight.
- The plant rooms' pumps, their local control panels, the boiler house's cascade and every control room instrument show the world's plant (`world-reading.ts`): the pump the world runs is the one that runs, on its isolators, its room's power and its trips; flows, levels, temperatures, chlorine, pH, currents and the annunciator's alarms are the world's. They had shown a scripted loop. Where there is no world (tests), the loop remains.
- The pool plant rooms' dosing controllers show their pool's free and combined chlorine, pH, redox and temperature; the hypochlorite and acid dosing pumps run (their LCDs and stroke lamps) while their pool is dosed and their store holds some; the UV unit's screen and lamps show its lamp on, or off, and UV FAIL with water flowing past an unlit lamp.
- The boiler house's twin-head LTHW circulator runs while the heating circuit is up, its duty head's display lit and heard (a small wet-rotor circulator's sound); the wet hall's make-up booster set is heard while it runs.
- A held sound asked for before the player's first click or key (a pump already running as its room is entered) now starts at that first gesture instead of being lost; and a pump stopped as it runs up no longer upsets its sound.
- The plant rooms' ozone monitor stands on its own galvanised post beside its generator, clear of the room's pipework (`ozone-monitor.ts`), its detector low, its supply in conduit from the post to the services ceiling; it had been stuck to the generator's end, unwired. The sand filters' gauges read their pool's pressures.
- Sectional tanks have a contents gauge, a sight tube on brackets on their face; the pool plant rooms' balance tanks and the wet hall's break and backwash tanks show their level in the world.
- A register of how every catalogued machine meets the world (`world-bindings.ts`), tested against the plant catalogue: each is wired, passive (with why) or still to do, a list that may only shrink.
- The circulation pumps are heard: a synthesised four-pole pump set (its motor's 100 Hz hum, its shaft's rumble, its impeller's blade-pass note, fan and water noise) held at whichever pump runs, rising as it runs up and falling as it coasts down.
- The plant rooms' ozone generators are in service: each has its own ozone plant, its MAINS on and started, its supply its isolator and its room's power in the world, restarting by itself when its supply returns (it had been the dev room's exhibit, off). Its ozone line leaves from its top, not through its front.
- The blockwork over and round doorways lines up with the wall beside it: every wall piece's texture is mapped from where it is in the room (`mapBlockwork`), in the plant rooms and the other service rooms alike.
- The switch-fuse opens: its door, hinged on its left and interlocked with its handle (it opens only at OFF, and the handle goes ON only with it shut), shows its incoming terminals, fuse base, three fuse carriers that pull out to change their fuses, and the neutral link; its internals are drawn only while the door is open. In every room, and on trial in dev0 and the electrical dev room.
- Every room's supply is switched by a Voltara switch-fuse on the wall by its entrance (side handle, padlockable OFF, SUPPLY ON neon, ROOM SUPPLY legend), replacing a placeholder of plain blocks; its conduit now comes down into its gland. Modelled first in the electrical dev room.
- Conduits end on the cable glands of the isolators they feed, instead of 2 cm above their boxes: the enclosures' sizes and glands are now one source (`enclosures.ts`) for their views and the routes to them.
- The footbath's make-up main runs overhead at 3.4 m, clear of head height and the equipment doorway, its valve worked from the floor by a chain wheel (shared now with the plant rooms' overhead valves).
- Lever-operated butterfly valves no longer flicker where the lever meets the stem: the stem runs up into a round boss at the lever's head, so no faces coincide.
- The MAIN PLANT sign over each pair of equipment doors stands above the concrete lintel instead of half behind it.
- The Jacuzzi's and the footbath's packaged cabinets start in service, their POWER on, so switching off their isolators is seen to stop them; the Jacuzzi plant room's mimic shows the world's circulation, water temperature and flow instead of fixed values.
- Electrical conduits in the rooms outside the pool plant are routed tidily: one distribution conduit round the walls on saddles, each panel's drop down the wall face into the top of the panel as drawn, chosen clear of the panels it would pass and of the other drops; a machine on the floor gets an outlet box on the wall above it and a flexible cable down to it.
- The control room's alarm annunciator hangs on the west wall toward the operator's desk, beside the pool mimic and over the water-test kit, in view and within reach (a wall panel marked `over` may now hang clear above a machine shallow enough to reach across).
- The control room's air-handling mimic no longer runs into the corner, and its title card, hidden behind the mimics, is gone.

### Added

- A port-graph feasibility trial with typed equipment and boundary ports, room and brand metadata, and validation examples; research notes distinguish installed ratings from live electrical, fluid, and heat quantities, with a prompt for a minimal coupled plant prototype.
- An independent review prompt for using a typed port graph as the installed-world contract, including visual and catalogue identity, child controls, port completeness, boundary nodes, line ratings, and simulation bindings.
- A walkable dev19 port-graph demo with visible fuel store, boiler, tank, pump, and electrical supply bound to simulation nodes and ports. Its controls affect finite fuel, water temperature, and accumulated electrical energy; invisible air and flue boundaries keep the topology explicit.
- The plant as one connected network, model first (`src/game/plant/network/`): the pools' water circuits, every machine and valve a node with its asset ID and tag, every pipe a link. Its water is solved, so valves and pumps change the flows: standby changeover, filter isolation, backwash, balance tank drain and pool drain-down all work on the model. Make-up (town main, break tank, booster set, meter, each plant room's branch) and drainage (each room's header, the drainage main, the backwash holding tank and its pump to the sewer) are in it too. Every node has a permanent serial number and a proximal ID (`pl2.f3.bv2`, its plate's tag in lowercase). What cannot yet be simulated is a placeholder, listed in `doc/reports/model-gaps.md`.
- Inventory entries now show physical component type and brand in the web and PDF schedules. Known installed makers come from their models; plain room valves and unresolved assemblies are explicitly `unbranded`. All 13 room supply switch-fuses now have permanent catalogue IDs, visible tags and live feeder states.
- An explicit installed equipment and valve register feeds the web inventory and the PDF manual. The schedule distinguishes simulated parts, saved controls whose circuit effect is pending, and placed equipment. The valve gap audit records the remaining machine-embedded controls.
- Pools can be drained down: each has drain-down and make-up valves in the world (`pool/drain-down`, `pool/make-up`); draining, its pumps take its water to the foul sewer at the site's consented 10 m³/h (shared with the backwash's discharge) down to its floor, and it closes to bathers once 15 cm down. Each hall's water shows the model's level: its surface, the tiles' waterline and caustics, the lane ropes and the gallery's view all go down as it drains, to a dry floor. The `drain-pool_0N` and `fill-pool_0N` scenarios (with `skip-N`) show it. Emptying one at a time: the Lounge about 14 hours, the Olympic about 11 days, the jacuzzi 18 minutes, the footbath 7.
- Behind the title screen, the Olympic hall live from 12 m up, at a quarter of the resolution and blurred, the camera in a slow 1° harmonic orbit about the hall's middle every 10 seconds; the game pauses underneath.
- A title screen on the front page: Boring Simulations Presents: The Pool, with New game (into the footbath), Continue (greyed out until the game has its own save), Settings and Instructions (the pause menu's tabs), worked by mouse, keys or gamepad, and the version and build in the corner. The developers' room list moves to `?dev`; Quit to Title returns to the title. The title is the default page: no arguments, or a room that does not exist, lands there (no longer the dev corridor).
- A 55-inch display as a component of its own (`display-screen.ts`): portrait or landscape, a slideshow cropped to fill its picture, and a red status lamp in its bottom right-hand corner; the rotunda's two displays use it, each fed from a switched fused spur mounted above it (the spur now a component of its own: logic, view with an engraved legend, and a working part), which switches the display off and on.
- A pause menu, opened by Esc once the mouse is free, a gamepad's Start, or the gear among each room's view tools: tabs for Game (Resume, Save, Quit to Title), Graphics, Audio and Controls (with the key and button list), worked by mouse, keys (Q and E switch tabs) or gamepad (d-pad, A, B, shoulder buttons). The world pauses while it is open; saving is continuous, Minecraft's way, and in a tab that only follows another with the game open, Save is greyed out and the menu says why. Its settings: master volume, look sensitivity and invert up and down (mouse, touch and gamepad), field of view, render quality (a 0 to 10 slider drawing 1 / ln(e + level) of full resolution, 1:1 at 0, applied at once, post-processing included) and on-screen controls, kept in the browser.
- The operator's manual, generated from the game's model (`npm run manual`): a LuaLaTeX PDF in the fiction's voice, from Bramhall Fenwick Engineering to Ashworth Borough Council, with Rowland Fenwick's foreword and a first chapter on the boiler house (its equipment plan and schedule, boiler settings, gas house schematic, supplies, start-up, gas emergency and signs).

### Removed

- From the spa cabinets, the parts that did nothing (the owner, 2026-10-25): the jacuzzi's internal surge vessel and the overflow pipe into it (its balance tank is outside), its pump's suction from that tank now coming in low through the back panel straight to the pump, its valve on the level length; and the old cartridge filter, replaced by the sand filter in both.
- The unused fluorescent ballast start-up recording, which was not cleared for distribution (the tubes' sounds are synthesised).

## [0.1.0] - 2026-10-22

The first release. It has no operator's manual yet: that comes in a later release (`doc/plans/operators-manual.md`).

### Added

- The leisure centre as one walkable building: four pool halls joined in a loop by tiled corridors, the plant rooms behind them, a main corridor, the switch room, boiler house, air handling plant, wet hall, chemical stores, loading bay, control room and engineer's office.
- A saved world state that runs the plant whichever room the player is in: water circulation and treatment, chemical dosing and stores, the wet hall's tanks and booster set, heating and the pools' thermal model against an outside temperature, lighting by zone, bathers who come and go by the timetable and the water's quality, and toxic gas detection for chlorine and ozone.
- Working machines and controls throughout, modelled on real products: switchboards, isolators, mimics, an annunciator, gauges, recorders, a scenario simulator, gas detectors and sounder-beacons.
- Modern safety signs and notices in place across the building.
- The development rooms, including dev2 Safety Systems and the corridor kit's rotunda in `corridor_lab`.
- Semantic versioning, this changelog and a release workflow.

### Fixed

- Walking from one room to the next no longer recompiles every shader of the room arrived in.
- The corridor posters load on the published site.
